Spherical higher order Fourier analysis over finite fields IV: an application to the Geometric Ramsey Conjecture Thanks: The author was partially supported by the NSF Grant DMS-2247331
Wenbo Sun

Abstract
. This paper is the fourth and the last part of the series Spherical higher order Fourier analysis over finite fields , aiming to develop the higher order Fourier analysis method along spheres over finite fields, and to solve the Geometric Ramsey Conjecture in the finite field setting. In this paper, we proof a conjecture of Graham on the Remsey properties for spherical configurations in the finite field setting. To be more precise, we show that for any spherical configuration X X of 𝔽 p d \mathbb{F}_{p}^{d} of complexity at most C C with d d being sufficiently large with respect to C C and | X | |X| , and for some prime p p being sufficiently large with respect to C C , | X | |X| and ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 , any set E ⊆ 𝔽 p d E\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d} with | E | > ϵ ​ p d |E|>\epsilon p^{d} contains at least ≫ C , ϵ , | X | p ( k + 1 ) ​ d − ( k + 1 ) ​ k / 2 \gg_{C,\epsilon,|X|}p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)k/2} congruent copies of X X , where k k is the dimension of span 𝔽 p ​ ( X − X ) \text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X) . The novelty of our approach is that we avoid the use of harmonic analysis, and replace it by the theory of spherical higher order Fourier analysis developed in previous parts of the series.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
05D10, 37A99

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Geometric Ramsey Theorem over finite fields

This paper is the forth and last part of the series
Spherical higher order Fourier analysis over finite fields

[
20
,
21
,
22
]
. The purpose of this paper is to use the tools developed in
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
to solve the Geometric Ramsey Conjecture in the finite field setting.
Geometric Ramsey theory is a topic first introduced in a series of papers by Erdös et al.
[
4
,
5
,
6
]
, which studies geometric patterns that can not be destroyed by partitioning Euclidean space into finitely many parts. We say that a finite set
X
is
geometrically Ramsey
if for every
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, there exists a dimension
d=d(r,X)
such that every
r
-coloring of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
contains a monochromatic and congruent copy of
\lambda X
for sufficiently large
\lambda>0
.
It was shown in
[
4
]
that geometrically Ramsey sets must be
spherical
, meaning that
E
is contained in the surface of a sphere. This led to a longstanding problem in combinatorics called the
Geometric Ramsey Conjecture
made by Graham
[
8
]
, saying that a finite set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is geometrically Ramsey if and only if it is spherical.

This conjecture is far from being settled and only partial results are known, including vertices of
k
-dimensional cubes
[
4
]
, non-degenerating simplices
[
1
,
7
]
(see also
[
16
]
), products of simplices
[
17
]
, trapezoids
[
14
]
, regular polygons and polyhedra
[
13
]
.

Since vector spaces over finite fields are useful models to study many problems in combinatorics and number theory, it is natural to ask whether Graham’s conjecture holds in this setting. We start with some definitions.
For
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d}),y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, define the
dot product
by
x\cdot y:=x_{1}y_{1}+\dots+x_{d}y_{d}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
and let
|x|^{2}:=x\cdot x
.
For convenience we call an ordered finite subset of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
to be a
configuration
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
Let

X=\{x_{j}\colon j\in J\}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
Y=\{y_{j}\colon j\in J\}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be two configurations in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
We say that
Y
is
congruent
to
X
if there exist an isometry
U
on
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
(i.e.
U\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is a linear transformation such that
|U(x)|^{2}=|x|^{2}
for all
x\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
) and
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
y_{j}=z+U(x_{j})
for all
j\in J
.
We say that
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is
spherical
if there exist
u_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
such that
|u-u_{0}|^{2}=r
for all
u\in X
.

Instead of studying the Geometric Ramsey Conjecture in the finite field setting, we formulate a density version of it as follows: for any spherical configuration
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and any subset
E\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
|E|>\epsilon p^{d}
,
E
contains a congruent copy of
X
if
p
is sufficiently large depending on
d
and
X
. We remark that the Geometric Ramsey Conjecture in the finite field setting fails for non-spherical configurations by Lemma 17 of
[
18
]
.

In this paper, we provide an affirmative answer to the density version of the Geometric Ramsey Conjecture in the finite field setting for general spherical configurations.
For any
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, let
k:=\dim(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X))
,
s:=|X|-k\geq 1
, and denote

(1.1)

\begin{split}d_{0}(X):=\max\{(2s+12)(15s+423),4k^{2}+12k+7\}.\end{split}

Theorem 1.1

(Density version of the Geometric Ramsey Theorem for finite fields)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
C,\epsilon>0
,
p
be a prime and
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be a spherical configuration of complexity at most
C
(see Definition
2.2
). Denote
k:=\dim(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X))
,
s:=|X|-k\geq 1
and suppose that
d\geq d_{0}(X)
. There exists
p_{0}=p_{0}(C,\epsilon,k,s)\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta=\delta(C,\epsilon,k,s)>0
such that if
p>p_{0}
, then every set
E\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
|E|>\epsilon p^{d}
contains at least
\delta p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)k/2}
congruent copies of
X
.

We remark that the quantity
p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)k/2}
arises from the observation that a random subset
E
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
contains approximately
\epsilon^{|X|}p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)k/2}
isometric copies of
X
(see Section
2.2
for the definition), where
k
is the dimension of
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X)
. This is because there are
k(k+1)/2
quadratic relations given by distances between the points in the generating set of
X
, which determines the locations of all other points in
X
, and each vertex is contained in
E
with probability
\epsilon
(see also page 2 of
[
18
]
).

The case
s=1
of Theorem
1.1
, where
X
is a simplex was proved by Parshall
[
19
]
(which can be viewed as an extension Bourgain’s work
[
1
]
). For the case
s=2
,
Theorem
1.1
was proved by Lyall, Magyar and Parshall
[
18
]
under the additional assumption that
X
is
non-degenerate
(meaning that
X-X
is a non-isotropic subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
).
1
1

1

The main result Theorem 1 in
[
18
]
was stated only for isometric copies, but its proof also applies to the case of congruent copies.
It is worth noting that the quantity
\delta
in the aforementioned results was independent of the complexity of
X
, whereas in Theorem
1.1
, unfortunately we were unable to drop the dependency on the complexity of the configuration. So it is natural to ask:

Question 1.2
.

Can we remove the dependence of
p_{0}
and
\delta
on
C
in Theorem
1.1
?

The lower bound of
d
we obtain in Theorem
1.1
is rather coarse. So it is also natural to ask:

Question 1.3
.

What is the optimal lower bound for
d
in Theorem
1.1
?

1.2.
Strategy of the proof

The study of the Geometric Ramsey Conjecture in literature uses methods from classical harmonic analysis. Relying on meticulous estimates on Fourier coefficients, such methods proved to be powerful when
s\leq 2
or when the configuration enjoys some extra symmetric properties. However, the harmonic analysis methods no longer work when
s\geq 3
. This is because the Fourier coefficient is insufficient to describe the spherical averages (see for example (
4.1
)) pertaining to this problem.

In this paper, instead of using harmonic analysis, we use the approach of the higher order Fourier analysis developed in the previous parts of the series
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
.
In Section
2
, we provide some basic algebraic properties which will be used in later sections. In Section
3
, by reducing Theorem
1.1
to Theorem
3.1
, we show that it suffices to consider the Geometric Ramsey Conjecture for subsets of a sphere.

The problem is then reduced to show the positivity of a spherical average of the form (
4.1
). Motivated by the work of Lyall, Magyar and Parshall
[
18
]
, we show in Section
4
that (
4.1
) can be bounded by the spherical Gowers norms of the functions
f_{i}
. Then in Section
5
, based on the work in the previous parts of the series
[
20
,
22
]
, we provide a structure theorem for spherical Gowers norms. It turns out that the factorization result in
[
20
]
is not good for our purposes, and so we provide a better version of it based on the idea of Green and Tao
[
9
]
.

The structure theorem essentially allows us to reduce the expression (
4.1
) to the case where
f_{i}
are sufficiently irrational nilsequences. In Section
6
, by developing the approach of Green and Tao
[
9
]
, we prove a joint equidistribution result (Theorem
6.5
) which can be used to deal with this case. Finally, in Section
7
, we combine all the results in previous sections to complete the proof of Theorem
3.1
and thus Theorem
1.1
.

In Appendix
A
, we recall some notations on polynomials and nilmanifolds defined in
[
20
]
. In Appendix
B
, we collect some results from previous parts of the series
[
20
,
22
]
which are used in this paper.

1.3.
Definitions and notations

Convention 1.4
.

Throughout this paper, we use

\tau\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\{0,\dots,p-1\}
to denote the natural bijective embedding, and use
\iota\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
to denote the map given by
\iota(n):=\tau^{-1}(n\mod p\mathbb{Z})
.
We also use

\tau
to denote the map from
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
to
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
given by
\tau(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}):=(\tau(x_{1}),\dots,\tau(x_{k}))
,
and

\iota
to denote the map from
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
to
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
given by
\iota(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}):=(\iota(x_{1}),\dots,

\iota(x_{k}))
.

We may also extend the domain of
\iota
to all the rational numbers of the form
x/y
with
(x,y)=1,x\in\mathbb{Z},y\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash p\mathbb{Z}
by setting
\iota(x/y):=\iota(xy^{\ast})
, where
y^{\ast}
is any integer with
yy^{\ast}\equiv 1\mod p\mathbb{Z}
.

Below are the notations we use in this paper:

•

Let
\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{+},\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}+,\mathbb{C}
denote the set of non-negative integers, positive integers, integers, rational numbers, real numbers, positive real numbers, and complex numbers, respectively. Denote
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\mathbb{F}_{p}
denote the finite field with
p
elements.

•

Throughout this paper,
d
is a fixed positive integer and
p
is a prime number.

•

Throughout this paper, unless otherwise stated, all vectors are assumed to be horizontal vectors.

•

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a collection of parameters and
A,B,c\in\mathbb{R}
. We write
A\gg_{\mathcal{C}}B
if
|A|\geq K|B|
and
A=O_{\mathcal{C}}(B)
if
|A|\leq K|B|
for some
K>0
depending only on the parameters in
\mathcal{C}
.
In the above definitions, we allow the set
\mathcal{C}
to be empty. In this case
K
will be a universal constant.

•

Denote
[p]:=\{0,\dots,p-1\}
,

•

For
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, denote
|i|:=|i_{1}|+\dots+|i_{k}|
.
For
n=(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, denote
n^{i}:=n_{1}^{i_{1}}\dots n_{k}^{i_{k}}
and
i!:=i_{1}!\dots i_{k}!
.
For
n=(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
i=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, denote
\binom{n}{i}:=\binom{n_{1}}{i_{1}}\dots\binom{n_{k}}{i_{k}}
.

•

We say that
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a
growth function
if
\mathcal{F}
is strictly increasing and
\mathcal{F}(n)\geq n
for all
n\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

•

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\lfloor x\rfloor
denote the largest integer which is not larger than
x
, and
\lceil x\rceil
denote the smallest integer which is not smaller than
x
. Let
\{x\}:=x-\lfloor x\rfloor
.

•

Let
X
be a finite set and
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
be a function. Denote
\mathbb{E}_{x\in X}f(x):=\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}f(x)
, the average of
f
on
X
.

•

We say that a set
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is
p
-periodic
if
\Omega=\Omega+p\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

•

For
F=\mathbb{Z}^{k}
or
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
, and
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}),y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})\in F
, let
x\cdot y\in\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{F}_{p}
denote the dot product given by

x\cdot y:=x_{1}y_{1}+\dots+x_{k}y_{k}.

•

If
G
is a connected, simply connected Lie group, then we use
\log G
to denote its Lie algebra. Let
\exp\colon\log G\to G
be the exponential map, and
\log\colon G\to\log G
be the logarithm map. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
g\in G
, denote
g^{t}:=\exp(t\log g)
.

•

If
f\colon H\to G
is a function from an abelian group
H=(H,+)
to some group
(G,\cdot)
, denote
\Delta_{h}f(n):=f(n+h)\cdot f(n)^{-1}
for all
n,h\in H
.

•

If
f\colon H\to\mathbb{C}
is a function on an abelian group
H=(H,+)
, denote
\Delta_{h}f(n):=f(n+h)\overline{f}(n)
for all
n,h\in H
.

•

We write affine subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
as
V+c
, where
V
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
passing through
\mathbb{0}
, and
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

•

There is a natural correspondence between polynomials taking values in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and polynomials taking values in
\mathbb{Z}/p
. Let
F\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}})
and
f\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{d^{\prime}})
be polynomials of degree at most
s
for some
s<p
. If
F=\iota\circ pf\circ\tau
, then we say that
F
is
induced
by
f
and
f
is a
lifting
of
F
.
2
2

2

As is explained in
[
20
]
,
\iota\circ pf\circ\tau
is well defined.

We say that
f
is a
regular lifting
of
F
if in addition
f
has the same degree as
F
and
f
has
\{0,\frac{1}{p},\dots,\frac{p-1}{p}\}
-coefficients.

•

Let
(X,d_{X})
be a metric space.
The
Lipschitz norm
of a function
F\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
is defined as

\|F\|_{\text{Lip}(X)}:=\sup_{x\in X}|F(x)|+\sup_{x,y\in X,x\neq y}\frac{|F(x)-F(y)|}{d_{X}(x,y)}.

When there is no confusion, we write
\|F\|_{\text{Lip}}=\|F\|_{\text{Lip}(X)}
for short.

Let
D,D^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
C>0
.
Here are some basic notions of complexities:

•

Real and complex numbers:
a number
r\in\mathbb{R}
is of
complexity
at most
C
if
r=a/b
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
with
-C\leq a,b\leq C
. If
r\notin\mathbb{Q}
, then we say that the complexity of
r
is infinity. A complex number is of
complexity
at most
C
if both its real and imaginary parts are of complexity at most
C
.

•

Vectors and matrices:
a vector or matrix is of
complexity
at most
C
if all of its entries are of complexity at most
C
.

•

Subspaces:
a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
is of
complexity
at most
C
if it is the null space of a matrix of complexity at most
C
.

•

Linear transformations:
let
L\colon\mathbb{C}^{D}\to\mathbb{C}^{D^{\prime}}
be a linear transformation. Then
L
is associated with an
D\times D^{\prime}
matrix
A
in
\mathbb{C}
. We say that
L
is of
complexity
at most
C
if
A
is of complexity at most
C
.

•

Lipschitz function:
the
complexity
of a Lipschitz function is defined to be its Lipschitz norm.

We also need to recall the notations regarding quadratic forms defined in
[
20
]
.

Definition 1.5
.

We say that a function
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a
quadratic form
if

M(n)=(nA)\cdot n+n\cdot u+v

for some
d\times d
symmetric matrix
A
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, some
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
We say that
A
is the matrix
associated to

M
.
We say that
M
is
pure
if
u=\mathbb{0}
.
We say that
M
is
homogeneous
if
u=\mathbb{0}
and
v=0
. We say that
M
is
non-degenerate
if
M
is of rank
d
, or equivalently,
\det(A)\neq 0
.

We use
\text{rank}(M):=\text{rank}(A)
to denote the
rank
of
M
.
Let
V+c
be an affine subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
of dimension
r
. There exists a (not necessarily unique) bijective linear transformation
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{r}\to V
.
We define the
rank

\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})
of
M
restricted to
V+c
as the rank of the quadratic form
M(\phi(\cdot)+c)
. It was proved in
[
20
]
that
\text{rank}(M|_{V+c})
is independent of the choice of
\phi
.

We also need to use the following notions.

•

For a polynomial
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{p})
, let
V(P)
denote the set of
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
such that
P(n)=0
.

•

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a quadratic form. Denote

V(M)^{h_{1},\dots,h_{r}}:=\cap_{i=1}^{r}(V(M(\cdot+h_{i}))\cap V(M).

•

Let
\Omega
be a subset of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

\Box_{s}(\Omega)
denote the set of
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{s+1}
such that
n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s}\in\Omega
for all
(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{s})\in\{0,1\}^{s}
.
Here we allow
s
to be 0, in which case
\Box_{0}(\Omega)=\Omega
.
We say that
\Box_{s}(\Omega)
is the
s
-th Gowers set
of
\Omega
.

Quadratic forms can also be defined in the
\mathbb{Z}/p
-setting.

Definition 1.6
.

We say that a function
M\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
is a
quadratic form
if

M(n)=\frac{1}{p}((nA)\cdot n+n\cdot u+v)

for some
d\times d
symmetric matrix
A
in
\mathbb{Z}
, some
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
v\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We say that
A
is the matrix
associated to

M
.

By Lemma A.1 of
[
20
]
,
any quadratic form
\tilde{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
associated with the matrix
\tilde{A}
induces a quadratic form
M:=\iota\circ p\tilde{M}\circ\tau\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
associated with the matrix
\iota(\tilde{A})
. Conversely, any quadratic form
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
associated with the matrix
A
admits a regular lifting
\tilde{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
, which is a quadratic form associated with the matrix
\tau(A)
.

For a quadratic form
\tilde{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}/p
, we say that
\tilde{M}
is
pure/homogeneous/
p
-non-degenerate
if the quadratic form
M:=\iota\circ p\tilde{M}\circ\tau
induced by
\tilde{M}
is pure/homogeneous/non-degenerate.
The
p
-rank
of
\tilde{M}
, denoted by
\text{rank}_{p}(\tilde{M})
, is defined to be the rank of
M
.

We say that
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
are
p
-linearly independent
if for all
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}/p
,
c_{1}h_{1}+\dots+c_{k}h_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}

implies that
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
, or equivalently, if
\iota(h_{1}),\dots,\iota(h_{k})
are linearly independent.

We also need to use the following notions.

•

For a polynomial
P\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\to\mathbb{R})
, let
V_{p}(P)
denote the set of
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
P(n+pm)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

•

For
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Box_{p,s}(\Omega)
denote the set of
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{s+1}
such that
n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s}\in\Omega+p\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for all
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{s}\in\{0,1\}
.
We say that
\Box_{p,s}(\Omega)
is the
s
-th
p
-Gowers set
of
\Omega
.

For all other definitions and notations which are used but not mentioned in this paper, we refer the readers to Appendices
A
and
B
for details.

2.
Algebraic structures for configurations

In this section, we provide some basic properties for various algebraic structures pertaining to the Geometric Ramsey Conjecture.

2.1.
Generating sets

Let

X=\{x_{j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon j\in J\}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be a configuration in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
J
being a finite ordered index set.
Let
I
be a subset of
J
. We say that
I
is a
generating set
of
X
if the vectors
x_{i}-x_{i_{0}},i\in I\backslash\{i_{0}\}
is a basis of
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X)
for some
i_{0}\in I
.
The following lemma is straightforward and we omit its proof.

Lemma 2.1
.

X=\{x_{j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\colon j\in J\}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be a configuration in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
J
being a finite ordered index set.

(1)

A subset
I\subseteq J
is a generating set of
X
if and only if the vectors
x_{i}-x_{i_{0}},i\in I\backslash\{i_{0}\}
is a basis of
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X)
for every
i_{0}\in I
. In particular, the cardinalities of generating sets of
X
are the same.

(2)

For every
j\in J
, there exists a generating set
I\subseteq J
of
X
containing
j
.

If
I
is a generating set of
X
, then it is clear that there exist unique
b_{I,i,j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p},i\in I,j\in J
such that
\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}=1
and
x_{j}=\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}x_{i}
for all
j\in J
.
We remark that
b_{I,i,j},i\in I,j\in J
satisfy the following
normalization property:

b_{I,i,j}=\delta_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in I
and
\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}=1
for all
j\in J
.

Let
L_{I,j}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|I|}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
denote the linear transformation given by

L_{I,j}(\mathbb{x}):=\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}x_{i},

and let
\mathbb{L}_{I}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|I|}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|J|}
denote the linear transformation given by

\mathbb{L}_{I}(\mathbb{x}):=(L_{I,j}(\mathbb{x}))_{j\in J}.

We say that
L_{I,j},j\in J
(or simply
\mathbb{L}_{I}
) are the
generating maps
for
X
with respect to
I
, and
b_{I,i,j},i\in I,j\in J
(or
b_{I,i},i\in I
) are the
generating constants
for
X
with respect to
I
.
We remark that the normalization property of
b_{I,i,j}
implies the following
normalization property
for
L_{I,j}
:

L_{I,j}((x_{i})_{i\in I})=x_{j}
for all
j\in I
and
L_{I,j}(x,\dots,x)=x
for all
j\in I
.

We are now ready to define the complexity of a configuration.

Definition 2.2

(Complexity of configurations)
.

Let
C>0
. We say that a configuration
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is of
complexity
at most
C
if there exists a generating set
I
of
X
such that all the generating constants for
X
with respect to
I
belong to
\{-C,\dots,C\}
.

In particular, if
X\subseteq\{-C,\dots,C\}^{d}
, then
X
is of complexity at most
O_{C,k}(1)
, where
k=\dim(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X))
.

Next we provide a change of variable property for generating maps.

Proposition 2.3

(Change of variable)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},

p
be a prime,
X
be a configuration in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
J
being a finite ordered index set, and
I,I^{\prime}\subseteq J
be two generating sets of
X
with generating maps
\mathbb{L}_{I}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|I|}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|J|}
and
\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|I^{\prime}|}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|J|}
respectively. Let
\pi_{I}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|J|}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|I|}
be the projection onto the coordinates with
I
-indices.
Then we have that

\mathbb{L}_{I}\circ\pi_{I}\circ\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}=\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}
. In particular, we have
\mathbb{L}_{I}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1})=\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1})
.

Proof.

Assume that
X=\{v_{j}\colon j\in J\}
and
let
b_{I,i,j},i\in I,j\in J
and
b_{I^{\prime},i,j},i\in I^{\prime},j\in J
be the generating constants for
X
with respect to
I
and
I^{\prime}
respectively.
We need to show that for all
j\in J
and
\mathbb{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|I^{\prime}|}
, we have that

\begin{split}L_{I,j}\circ\pi_{I}\circ\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}(\mathbb{x})=L_{I^{\prime},j}(\mathbb{x}).\end{split}

For any
j\in J
, we have that

\begin{split}\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},j}v_{i^{\prime}}=v_{j}=\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}v_{i}=\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},i}v_{i^{\prime}}=\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}\Bigl(\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},i}\Bigr)v_{i^{\prime}},\end{split}

or equivalently,

(2.1)

\begin{split}\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}\Bigl(b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},j}-\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},i}\Bigr)v_{i^{\prime}}=\mathbb{0}.\end{split}

On the other hand, by the normalization property of
b_{I,i,j}
,

(2.2)

\begin{split}\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}\Bigl(b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},j}-\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},i}\Bigr)=\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},j}-\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}\Bigl(\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},i}\Bigr)=1-\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}=0.\end{split}

Since
I^{\prime}
is a generating set of
X
, the vectors
v_{i^{\prime}}-v_{i_{0}},i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}\backslash\{i_{0}\}
are linearly independent for some
i_{0}\in I^{\prime}
. It then follows from (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) that

(2.3)

\begin{split}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},j}=\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},i}\end{split}

for all
j\in J
. So

\begin{split}&\quad L_{I,j}\circ\pi_{I}\circ\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}(\mathbb{x})=\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}(L_{I^{\prime},i}(\mathbb{x}))=\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},i}x_{i^{\prime}}\\
&=\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}\Bigl(\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},i}\Bigr)x_{i^{\prime}}=\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime},j}x_{i^{\prime}}=L_{I^{\prime},j}(\mathbb{x}).\end{split}

We now prove that
\mathbb{L}_{I}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1})=\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1})
. Note that

\mathbb{L}_{I}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1})=\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}\circ\pi_{I^{\prime}}\circ\mathbb{L}_{I}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1})\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}).

Similarly,
\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1})\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{I}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}).
This implies that
\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1})=\mathbb{L}_{I}((\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}).

∎

2.2.
M
-spherical sets

In this paper,
instead of working with the inner product
|\cdot|^{2}
, it is convenient to consider more general quadratic forms.
Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a homogeneous quadratic form. We say that a set
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is
M
-spherical
if there exist
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
such that
M(x-z)=r
for all
x\in X
. It is not hard to see that every simplex is
M
-spherical:

Lemma 2.4

(Simplices are
M
-spherical)
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate homogeneous quadratic form and
X=\{x_{i}\colon i\in J\}
be a configuration in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Suppose that for some
i_{0}\in J
, the vectors
x_{i}-x_{i_{0}},i\in J\backslash\{i_{0}\}
are linearly independent. Then
X
is
M
-spherical.

Proof.

It suffices to show that there exists
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
M(x_{i}-z)=M(x_{i_{0}}-z)
for all
i\in J\backslash\{i_{0}\}
. This is equivalent of solving the linear equation system
((x_{j}-x_{j_{0}})A)\cdot z=b_{j},j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}
, where
A
is the matrix associated to
M
and
b_{j}
is some constant depending on
M
and
X
. Since
x_{j}-x_{j_{0}},j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}
are linearly independent, so are
(x_{j}-x_{j_{0}})A,j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}
since
A
is invertible. So such
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
exists by the knowledge of linear algebra, which implies that
X
is
M
-spherical.
∎

The following is a convenient criterion for
M
-spherical sets.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate homogeneous quadratic form.
A configuration
X=\{x_{j}\colon j\in J\}
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is
M
-spherical if and only if for any quadratic form
M^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
whose associated matrix is the same as
M
, and any
c_{j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p},j\in J
, if
\sum_{j\in J}c_{j}=0,

\sum_{j\in J}c_{j}x_{j}=\mathbb{0}
, then
\sum_{j\in J}c_{j}M^{\prime}(x_{j})=0
.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to Lemma 16 of
[
18
]
and we write it down for completeness. Assume that
M(n)=(nA)\cdot n
and
that
M^{\prime}(n)=(nA)\cdot n+u\cdot n+v
for some
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, where
A
is the matrix associated to
M
and
M^{\prime}
.
If
X
is
M
-spherical, then
M(x_{j}-z)=r
for some
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
j\in J
. So

M(x_{j})=r-M(z)+x_{j}\cdot(2zA)\text{ and }M^{\prime}(x_{j})=r-M(z)+v+x_{j}\cdot(2zA+2u).

Therefore,

\sum_{j\in J}c_{j}M^{\prime}(x_{j})=\sum_{j\in J}c_{j}(r-M(z)+v)+\sum_{j\in J}(c_{j}x_{j})\cdot(2zA+2u)=0.

Conversely, suppose that
X
is not
M
-spherical. We wish to construct
c_{j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p},j\in J
such that
\sum_{j\in J}c_{j}=0
,
\sum_{i=0}^{k}c_{i}x_{i}=\mathbb{0}
, but
\sum_{i=0}^{k}c_{i}M^{\prime}(x_{i})\neq 0
. We may assume without loss of generality that
X
is minimal in the sense that any proper subset of
X
is
M
-spherical. Fix any
j_{0}\in J
.
Since
X
is not
M
-spherical, by
Lemma
2.4
,
x_{j}-x_{j_{0}},j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}
are linearly dependent.
So we may assume that
\sum_{j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}}a_{j}(x_{j}-x_{j_{0}})=\mathbb{0}
for some
a_{j}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
not all equal to 0. Assume that
a_{j_{1}}\neq 0
for some
j_{1}\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}
.

Let
X^{\prime}
denote the configuration
\{x_{j}\colon j\in J\backslash\{j_{1}\}\}
. By the minimality of
X
,
X^{\prime}
is
M
-spherical.
So
M(x_{j}-z)=r
for some
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
j\in J\backslash\{j_{1}\}
. Since
X
is not
M
-spherical,
M(x_{j_{1}}-z)-r=t\neq 0
. It is not hard to see that

M(x_{j})-M(x_{j_{0}})=(x_{j}-x_{j_{0}})\cdot(2zA)

for all
j\in J\backslash\{j_{0},j_{1}\}
, and

M(x_{j_{1}})-M(x_{j_{0}})=(x_{j_{1}}-x_{j_{0}})\cdot(2zA)+t.

Since
\sum_{j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}}a_{j}(x_{j}-x_{j_{0}})=0
, we have that

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}}a_{j}(M^{\prime}(x_{j})-M^{\prime}(x_{j_{0}}))=\sum_{j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}}a_{j}(M(x_{j})-M(x_{j_{0}}))\\
&=\sum_{j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}}a_{j}(x_{j}-x_{j_{0}})\cdot(2zA)+a_{j_{1}}t=a_{j_{1}}t\neq 0.\end{split}

We are done by taking
c_{j_{0}}=-\sum_{j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}}a_{j}
and
c_{j}=a_{j}
for all
j\in J\backslash\{j_{0}\}
.
∎

As a corollary of Lemma
2.5
, we have the following generalization of Lemma 5 of
[
18
]
:

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate homogeneous quadratic form and
X=\{x_{j}\colon j\in J\}
be an
M
-spherical configuration in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Let
I
be a generating set of
X
with
L_{I,j},j\in J
be its generating maps. If
M(y_{i}-z)=r
for some
z,y_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
i\in I
, then
M(L_{I,j}((y_{i})_{i\in I})-z)=r
for all
j\in J
.

In particular, if
M(x_{i}-z)=r
for some
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
i\in I
, then
M(x_{j}-z)=r
for all
j\in J
.

Proof.

For convenience denote
M^{\prime}(n):=M(n-z)-r
and
y_{j}:=L_{I,j}((y_{i})_{i\in I})
for all
j\in J\backslash I
. Recall that we also have
L_{I,j}((y_{i})_{i\in I})=y_{j}
for all
j\in I
. Then

M^{\prime}(y_{i})=0
for all
i\in I
.
Let
b_{I,i,j},i\in I,j\in J
be the generating constants for
X
with respect to
I
. Then for all
j\in J\backslash I
,
\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}-1=0
and
\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}y_{i}-y_{j}=0
.
Since
X
is
M
-spherical,
By Lemma
2.5
,
\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}M^{\prime}(y_{i})-M^{\prime}(y_{j})=0
. So
M^{\prime}(y_{j})=0
for all
j\in J\backslash I
.

The “in particular” part follows from the fact that
x_{j}:=L_{I,j}((x_{i})_{i\in I})
for all
j\in J
.
∎

2.3.
Different notions of congruency

In order to prove Theorem
1.1
, instead of working directly on congruent copies of a configuration, we study relavent patterns with less restrictions.

Definition 2.7

(
M
-isometry and
M
-congruency)
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a homogeneous quadratic form
and

X=\{x_{j}\colon j\in J\}
,
Y=\{y_{j}\colon j\in J\}
be two configurations in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. We say that
X
and
Y
are
M
-isometric
if
M(x_{i}-x_{j})=M(y_{i}-y_{j})
for all
i,j\in J
.
We say that
Y
is
M
-congruent
to
X
if there exist an
M
-isometry

U
on
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
(i.e.
U\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is a linear transformation such that
M(U(x))=M(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
) and
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
y_{j}=z+U(x_{j})
for all
j\in J
.

When
M=|\cdot|^{2}
, we simply call
M
-isometry/congruency as
isometry/congruency
for short.

It is not hard to see that congruency implies isometry. However, the next example shows that the converse is false.

Example 2.8
.

Let
d=5
,
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{4}\}
and
Y=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{4}\}
with
x_{1}=y_{1}=(-1,-1,0,0,

0)
,
x_{2}=y_{2}=(-1,1,0,0,0)
,
x_{3}=y_{3}=(1,-1,0,0,0)
,
x_{4}=(1,1,0,0,0)
, and
y_{4}=(1,1,a,b,c)
, where
a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}=0
and
(a,b,c)\neq(0,0,0)
. Such
a,b,c
exist because of Lemma
B.5
. Then
X
and
Y
are isometric. It is clear that
X
and
Y
are spherical since
|x_{i}|^{2}=|y_{i}|^{2}=2
.

3
3

3

Moreover,
X
is non-degenerate.
On the other hand, if there exist an isometry
U
on
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
y_{i}=z+U(x_{i})
for all
1\leq i\leq 4
, then

\begin{split}&\quad(0,0,a,b,c)=y_{1}+y_{4}-y_{2}-y_{3}=(z+U(x_{1}))+(z+U(x_{4}))-(z+U(x_{2}))-(z+U(x_{3}))\\
&=U(x_{1}+x_{4}-x_{2}-x_{3})=(0,0,0,0,0),\end{split}

a contradiction. So
Y
is not congruent to
X
.

Next we introduce a variation of the
M
-congruency concept.

Definition 2.9

(almost
M
-congruency)
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a homogeneous quadratic form
and

X=\{x_{j}\colon j\in J\}
and
Y=\{y_{j}\colon j\in J\}
be two configurations in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
Let
I\subseteq J
be a generating set of
X
with generating constants
b_{I,i,j},i\in I,j\in J
.
We say that
Y
is
almost
M
-congruent
to
X
with respect to
I
if

•

for any
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
M(x_{i}-x_{i^{\prime}})=M(y_{i}-y_{i^{\prime}})
(i.e.
\{x_{i}\colon i\in I\}
is
M
-isometric to
\{y_{i}\colon i\in I\}
);

•

for any
i\in J\backslash I
,
y_{j}=\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}y_{i}
.
4
4

4

We also have
y_{j}=\sum_{i\in I}b_{I,i,j}y_{i}
for
i\in I
for free, since
b_{I,i,j}=\delta_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in I
.

We say that
Y
is
almost
M
-congruent
to
X
if
Y
is almost
M
-congruent to
X
with respect to every generating set of
X
. When
M=|\cdot|^{2}
, we simply call almost
M
-congrency as
almost congrency
for short.

Roughly speaking, almost congruency is a mixture of an isometry property for a generating set of the configuration, and some linearity property for the remaining points. The concept of almost congruency was used implicitly in the work of Lyall, Magyar and Parshall
[
18
]
, and it is very useful in converting the Geometric Ramsey Conjecture to the study of linear patterns in spheres. We provide some connections between different notions of congruency for later uses.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate homogeneous quadratic form,
X=\{x_{j}\colon j\in J\}
and
Y=\{y_{j}\colon j\in J\}
be two configurations, and
I\subseteq J
be a generating set
I
of
X
.

(1)

If
Y
is
M
-congruent to
X
, then
Y
is almost
M
-congruent and
M
-isometric to
X
.

(2)

If
Y
is almost
M
-congruent to
X
with respect to
I
, and if the vectors
y_{i}-y_{i_{0}},i\in I\backslash\{i_{0}\}
are linearly independent for some
i_{0}\in I
,
5
5

5

When
Y
is almost
M
-congruent to
X
, this is equivalent of saying that
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X)
and
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(Y-Y)
have the same dimension.
then

Y
is
M
-congruent to
X
(and thus is almost
M
-congruent to
X
with respect to every generating set of
X
by Part (i)). If in addition
X
is
M
-spherical, then so is
Y
.

Proof.

Part (i) is straightforward.
We now prove Part (ii).
Let
X^{\prime}=\{x_{j}\colon j\in I\}
and
Y^{\prime}=\{y_{j}\colon j\in I\}
.
By linearity, it suffices to show that
X^{\prime}
is
M
-congruent to
Y^{\prime}
. For convenience assume without loss of generality that
I=\{0,\dots,k\}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+},
that
i_{0}=0
and that
x_{0}=y_{0}=\mathbb{0}
. Then
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
and
(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})
are linearly independent tuples. Let
U=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}
and
V=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\}
. Then
x_{i}\mapsto y_{i},1\leq i\leq k
induces a bijective linear transformation
\phi\colon U\to V
. Let
A
be the matrix associated to
M
.
Since
Y
is almost
M
-congruent to
X
, we have that

M(y_{i}-y_{j})=M(x_{i}-x_{j})
for all
1\leq i,j\leq k
and that
M(y_{i})=M(x_{i})
for all
1\leq i\leq k
(since
x_{0}=y_{0}=\mathbb{0}
). From this it is not hard to see that
(x_{i}A)\cdot x_{j}=(y_{i}A)\cdot y_{j}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq k
, which implies that

M(a_{1}x_{1}+\dots+a_{k}x_{k})=M(a_{1}y_{1}+\dots+a_{k}y_{k})

for all
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
. In other words,
\phi
is a bijective
M
-isometry. Since
M
is non-degenerate, by Witt’s Extension Theorem (see for example
[
3
]
Corollary 24),
\phi
extension to an
M
-isometry from
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. So
X^{\prime}
is
M
-congruent to
Y^{\prime}
, and thus
X
is
M
-congruent to
Y
.

We now assume further that
X
is
M
-spherical and show that
Y
is also
M
-spherical. Since
Y
is almost
M
-congruent to
X
, by Corollary
2.6
, it suffices to show that
Y^{\prime}
is spherical,
which follows from Lemma
2.4
.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

A result similar to Proposition
2.10
was proven in Lemma 14
[
18
]
under the additional assumption that
X
is non-degenerate. In fact, the assumptions in both results share the same purpose in ensuring

\dim(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X))=\dim(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(Y-Y))
, from which one can apply Witt’s Extension Theorem to show the existence of the
M
-isometry
\phi
.

3.
Some preliminary reductions of the density Ramsey theorem

3.1.
A variation of Theroem
1.1
over spheres

In this section, we reduce Theorem
1.1
to the following result:

Theorem 3.1

(Density Ramsey Theorem on spheres)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
C,\epsilon>0
,
p
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate homogeneous quadratic form, and
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be an
M
-spherical configuration of complexity at most
C
. Let
k
be the dimension of
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X)
and suppose that
d\geq d_{0}(X)
. There exist
p_{0}=p_{0}(C,d,\epsilon)\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta=\delta(C,d,\epsilon)>0
such that if
p>p_{0}
, then for every
x\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, every
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
and every set
E\subseteq V(M(\cdot+x)-r)
(recall Section
1.3
for the definition) with
|E|>\epsilon p^{d-1}
,
E
contains at least
\delta p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)(k+2)/2}
almost
M
-congruent copies of
X
.

Remark 3.2
.

Note that Theorem
3.1
differs from Theorem
1.1
in the following aspects. Firstly, the set
E
in Theorem
3.1
is restricted to the “sphere”
V(M(\cdot+x)-r)
. Secondly, the quantity
\delta
in Theorem
3.1
is allowed to be dependent on
d
(and thus depends on
|X|
implicitly since
d\geq d_{0}(X)
). Finally, Theorem
3.1
deals with

M
-spherical sets and almost
M
-congruent copies for a generate non-degenerate homogeneous quadratic form
M
.

In order to see the connection between Theorems
1.1
and
3.1
, it is convenient to consider the following intermediate result:

Proposition 3.3

(Density Ramsey Theorem, a weaker form)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
C,\epsilon>0
,
p
be a prime,
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a non-degenerate homogeneous quadratic form, and
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be an
M
-spherical configuration of complexity at most
C
. Let
k
be the dimension of
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X)
and suppose that
d\geq d_{0}(X)
. There exist
p_{0}=p_{0}(C,d,\epsilon)\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta=\delta(C,d,\epsilon)>0
such that if
p>p_{0}
, then every set
E\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
|E|>\epsilon p^{d}
contains at least
\delta p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)k/2}

M
-congruent copies of
X
.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
assuming Theorem
3.1
.

For convenience denote

M_{x,r}(n):=M(n+x)-r.

Let
s:=|X|-k\geq 1
and
E\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
|E|>\epsilon p^{d}
.
Assume without loss of generality that
X=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{k+s-1}\}
and
\{0,\dots,k\}
is a generating set of
X
.
Note that for any
n\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, the number of tuples
(x,r)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d+1}
such that
M_{x,r}(n)=0
is
p^{d}
(since
r
is uniquely determined by
x
and
n
). So
\sum_{(x,r)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d+1}}|E\cap V(M_{x,r})|=p^{d}|E|>\epsilon p^{2d}.
Since
|E\cap V(M_{x,r})|\leq|V(M_{x,r})|\leq 2p^{d-1}
, by the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists a subset
W
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d+1}
of cardinality
\gg\epsilon p^{d+1}
such that
|E\cap V(M_{x,r})|\gg\epsilon p^{d-1}
for all
(x,r)\in W
. By Theorem
3.1
, for all
(x,r)\in W
, the set
E\cap V(M_{x,r})
contains
\gg_{C,d,\epsilon}p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)(k+2)/2}
almost
M
-congruent copies
Y=\{v_{0},\dots,v_{k+s-1}\}
of
X
.

By Lemma
B.3
, the number of choices of
v_{0},\dots,v_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
v_{1}-v_{0},\dots,v_{k}-v_{0}
are linearly dependent is at most
kp^{d+(d+1)(k-1)}=kp^{dk+(k-1)}
. Since
d\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+k
, the set
E\cap V(M_{x,r})
contains
\gg_{C,d,\epsilon}p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)(k+2)/2}
almost
M
-congruent copies
Y=\{v_{0},\dots,v_{k+s-1}\}
of
X
such that
v_{1}-v_{0},\dots,v_{k}-v_{0}
are linearly independent (because
v_{k+1},\dots,v_{k+s-1}
are uniquely determined by
v_{0},\dots,v_{k}
).

Fix any such
Y
. By Proposition
2.10
(ii),
Y
is
M
-congruent to
X
. Since
\{0,\dots,k\}
is a generating set of
X
, by Corollary
2.6
,

Y\subseteq V(M_{x,r})
if and only if

v_{0},\dots,v_{k}
belong to
V(M_{x,r})
, or equivalently,
x\in V(M_{v_{0},r})^{v_{1}-v_{0},\dots,v_{k}-v_{0}}
(recall Section
1.3
for the definition). Since
v_{1}-v_{0},\dots,v_{k}-v_{0}
are linearly independent,
and
d\geq 2k+3
, by Lemma
B.6
,
|V(M_{v_{0},r})^{v_{0}-v_{1},\dots,v_{0}-v_{k}}|=p^{d-k-1}(1+O(p^{-1/2}))
.
Since there are
p
choices of
r
, the number of
(x,r)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d+1}
such that
Y\subseteq V(M_{x,r})
is at most
2p^{(d+1)-(k+1)}
if
p\gg_{d}1
.

So the total number of
M
-congruent copies of
X
is no less than the cardinality of the set
E=\cup_{(x,r)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d+1}}(E\cap V(M_{x,r}))
, which is at least

\gg_{C,d,\epsilon}p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)(k+2)/2}\cdot|W|/2p^{(d+1)-(k+1)}\gg_{C,d,\epsilon}p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)k/2}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
assuming Proposition
3.3
.

Let
X=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{k+s-1}\}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
be a spherical configuration of complexity at most
C
, where
\dim(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X))=k
and
s=|X|-k\geq 1
.
Assume without loss of generality that
I:=\{0,\dots,k\}
is a generating set of
X
and let
b_{I,i,j},i\in I,j\in\{0,\dots,k+s-1\}
be its generating constants.
Let
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
,
x\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, and
E\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
|E|>\epsilon p^{d}
.
Suppose that
d\geq d_{0}:=d_{0}(X)
. Throughout the proof we assume that
p\gg_{C,d_{0},\epsilon}1
.

Our goal is to show that the dependences of
p_{0}
and
\delta
on
d
in Proposition
3.3
can be replaced by the dependences on
k
and
s
. Let
M\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be the quadratic form given by
M(x):=x\cdot x
. We say that a basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is
good
if
\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}}\}
is not
M
-isotropic (see Appendix
B.1
for the definition).
Fix any good basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
. Denote
V:=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}}\}
and
U:=\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{v_{d_{0}+1},\dots,v_{d}\}
. Then
M|_{V+c}
is of full rank for all
c\in U
. By the Pigeonhole Principle, there exists a subset
U^{\prime}(v_{1},\dots,v_{d})
of
U
of cardinality at least
\epsilon p^{d-d_{0}}/2
such that for all
c\in U^{\prime}(v_{1},\dots,v_{d})
, we have that
|E\cap(V+c)|>\epsilon p^{d_{0}}/2
.

Fix such an element
c
and
let
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{0}}\to V
be any bijective linear transformation. Denote
M^{\prime}=M\circ\phi
. Then
M^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{0}}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a non-degenerate homogeneous quadratic form. Let
E^{\prime}:=\{m\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{0}}\colon\phi(m)+c\in E\}
. Then
|E^{\prime}|>\epsilon p^{d_{0}}/2
.

Let
W
be the set of
(y_{0},\dots,y_{k})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{0}})^{k+1}
such that
M^{\prime}(y_{i}-y_{i^{\prime}})=M(x_{i}-x_{i^{\prime}})
for all
0\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq k
. Using Proposition
B.11
, it is not hard to check that
W
is a consistent
M^{\prime}
-set
of total co-dimension
k(k+1)/2
(see Appendix
B.3
for definitions).
By Theorem
B.12
, since
\text{rank}(M^{\prime})=d_{0}\geq k^{2}+k+1
, we have that
|W|=p^{d_{0}(k+1)-k(k+1)/2}(1+O_{k}(p^{-1/2}))
. On the other hand, by Lemma
B.3
, the number of
(y_{0},\dots,y_{k})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{0}})^{k+1}
such that
y_{1}-y_{0},\dots,y_{k}-y_{0}
are linearly dependent is at most
kp^{d_{0}+(d_{0}+1)(k-1)}=kp^{d_{0}k+(k-1)}
. Since
d_{0}\geq\frac{k(k+1)}{2}+k
and
p\gg_{d_{0},k}1
, there exists some configuration
Y=\{y_{0},\dots,y_{k+s-1}\}
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d^{\prime}}
such that
M^{\prime}(y_{i}-y_{i^{\prime}})=M(x_{i}-x_{i^{\prime}})
for all
0\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq k
, that

y_{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}b_{I,i,j}y_{i}
for all
j\in\{0,\dots,k+s-1\}
,
and that
y_{1}-y_{0},\dots,y_{k}-y_{0}
are linearly independent. Therefore,
I
is a generating set of
Y
. Since
\phi
is injective,
I
is also a generating set of
\phi(Y)+c
.

Note that

M((\phi(y_{i})+c)-(\phi(y_{i^{\prime}})+c))=M^{\prime}(y_{i}-y_{i^{\prime}})=M(x_{i}-x_{i^{\prime}})

for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and that

\phi(y_{j})+c=\sum_{i=0}^{k}b_{I,i,j}(\phi(y_{i})+c)

for all
j\in\{0,\dots,k+s-1\}
. We have that
\phi(Y)+c
is an almost
M
-congruent copy of
X
with respect to
I
. By Proposition
2.10
(ii),
\phi(Y)+c
is
M
-spherical.
By Lemma
2.4
,
\{y_{i}\colon i\in I\}
is
M^{\prime}
-spherical. Assume that
M^{\prime}(y_{i}-z)=r
for some
z\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d_{0}}
and
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
i\in I
.
Then
M((\phi(y_{i})+c)-(\phi(z)+c))=r
for all
i\in I
. Since
I
is a generating set of the
M
-spherical configuration
\phi(Y)+c
, by Corollary
2.6
,
M((\phi(y_{i})+c)-(\phi(z)+c))=r
and thus
M^{\prime}(y_{j}-z)=r
for all
j\in\{0,\dots,k+s-1\}
. So

Y
is
M^{\prime}
-spherical.

Since
p\gg_{C,d_{0},\epsilon}1
,by Theorem
3.3
,
E^{\prime}
contains at least
\delta p^{(k+1)d_{0}-(k+1)k/2}

M^{\prime}
-congruent copies of
Y
, where
\delta:=\delta(C,d_{0},\epsilon)>0
. Composing with
\phi
, it is not hard to see that
E\cap(V+c)
contains at least
\delta p^{(k+1)d_{0}-(k+1)k/2}

M
-congruent copies of
\phi(Y)+c
.
Ranging over all
c\in U^{\prime}(v_{1},\dots,v_{d})
, we have that
E
contains at least
\delta\epsilon p^{(d-d_{0})+(k+1)d_{0}-(k+1)k/2}/4

M
-congruent copies of
X
with respect to any good basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.

Let
B
be the
d\times d
matrix with
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
being its row vectors, and
B^{\prime}
be the
d_{0}\times d
matrix with
v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}}
being its row vectors. Then
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
is a good basis of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
if any only if
\det(B),\det(B^{\prime}{B^{\prime}}^{T})\neq 0
. By Lemma
B.4
, the number of good basis of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is
p^{d^{2}}(1+O_{d}(p^{-1}))
.
Therefore, letting
R
denote the set of tuples
(X^{\prime},v_{1},\dots,v_{d},c)
with
X^{\prime}
being
M
-congruent to
X
,
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
being a good basis of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
, and
c\in U^{\prime}(v_{1},\dots,v_{d})
, we have that

(3.1)

|R|\geq\delta\epsilon p^{d^{2}+(d-d_{0})+(k+1)d_{0}-(k+1)k/2}/8.

Claim.

For any
1\leq t\leq d_{0}
and
u_{0},\dots,u_{t}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
with
u_{1}-u_{0},\dots,u_{t}-u_{0}
being linearly independent, the number of tuples
(v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{d_{0}}
such that
u_{0},\dots,u_{t}\in\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}}\}
is at most
p^{d_{0}t+d(d_{0}-t)}
.

Denote
u^{\prime}_{i}:=u_{i}-u_{0}
for
1\leq i\leq t
.
Note that
u_{0},\dots,u_{t}\in\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}}\}
only if
u^{\prime}_{1},\dots,u^{\prime}_{t}\in\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}}\}
. Assume that

u^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{0}}c_{i,j}v_{j}
for all
1\leq i\leq t
. There are at most
p^{td_{0}}
choices of
c_{i,j}
. For any choice of
c_{i,j}
, to achieve
u^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{0}}c_{i,j}v_{j},1\leq i\leq t
, we must have that
(c_{i,1},\dots,c_{i,d_{0}}),1\leq i\leq t
are linearly independent. If this is the case, then there exists a subset
I
of
\{1,\dots,d_{0}\}
with
|I|=t
such that
v_{j},j\in I
are uniquely determined by
u_{i},v_{j^{\prime}},c_{i,j},1\leq i\leq t,1\leq j\leq d_{0},j^{\prime}\notin I
. Since there are
p^{d(d_{0}-t)}
choices of
v_{j^{\prime}},j^{\prime}\notin I
, we conclude that the number of tuples
(v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}})
such that
u^{\prime}_{1},\dots,u^{\prime}_{t}\in\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}}\}
is at most
p^{d_{0}t+d(d_{0}-t)}
. This proves the claim.

Fix any
M
-congruent copy
X^{\prime}
of
X
and any
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
Since
|X^{\prime}|=k+1
and

\dim(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}((X^{\prime}-c)-(X^{\prime}-c)))=\dim(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X^{\prime}-X^{\prime}))=\dim(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X))=k,

by the claim, the number of tuples
(v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}})
such that
X^{\prime}-c\subseteq\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{d_{0}}\}
is at most
p^{d_{0}k+d(d_{0}-k)}
.
So each
X^{\prime}
appears at most
p^{d(d-d_{0})+d_{0}k+d(d_{0}-k)}
times in the set
R
.
So it follows from (
3.1
)
that
E
contains in total at least

\delta\epsilon p^{d^{2}+(d-d_{0})+(k+1)d_{0}-(k+1)k/2-(d(d-d_{0})+d_{0}k+d(d_{0}-k))}/8=\delta\epsilon p^{(k+1)d-(k+1)k/2}/8

M
-congruent (and thus congruent) copies of
X
. This completes the proof.
∎

In conclusion, in order to prove Theorem
1.1
, it suffices to prove Theorem
3.1
.

3.2.
Definitions and conventions

The rest of the paper is devoted to proving Theorem
3.1
.
We start with some simplifications and definitions.

In the rest of the paper,
we fix the non-degenerate homogeneous quadratic form
M
, the matrix
A
associated to it, and the radius
r\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
We fix an
M
-spherical configuration
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}

and denote
k:=\dim(\text{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(X-X)),s:=|X|-k\geq 1
.

In the rest of the paper, we label the coordinates of vectors in the sets
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{t})^{k+1}
and
(\mathbb{Z}^{t})^{k+1}
as
0\sim k
instead of
1\sim(k+1)
, and label the coordinates of vectors in the sets
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{t})^{k+s}
and
(\mathbb{Z}^{t})^{k+s}
as
0\sim(k+s-1)
instead of
1\sim(k+s)
.
Under this convention,
from now on we assume that
J=\{0,\dots,k+s-1\}
is the index set for
X
with respect to the canonical ordering.
For each generating set
I
of
X
, let
\mathbb{L}_{I}=(L_{I,0},\dots,L_{I,k+s-1})
be the generating map of
X
with respect to
I
with generating constants
b_{I,i,j},i\in I,j\in J
, and let
\pi_{I}\colon(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|J|}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|I|}
be the projection onto the coordinates with
I
-indices.

Replacing
X
with
X-u_{0}
if necessary, we may further assume that

X=\{v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{k+s-1}\}

with
M(v_{0})=\dots=M(v_{k+s-1})=\lambda
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Note that if
E\subseteq V(M(\cdot+x)-r)
, then
E+x\subseteq V(M-r)
. Since the number of almost
M
-congruent copies of
X
in
E
is the same as that of
E+r
, we may assume without loss of generality that
x=\mathbb{0}
and
E\subseteq V(M-r)
.

In the rest of the paper, for any
I\subseteq L
, we use
\Omega_{I}
to denote the set of
(x_{i})_{i\in I}\in V(M-r)^{|I|}
such that
M(x_{i}-x_{i^{\prime}})=M(v_{i}-v_{i^{\prime}})
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
. Also denote

\Omega:=\mathbb{L}_{I}(\Omega_{I})
for some generating
I
of
X
.
We summarize some properties for the sets
\Omega_{I}
and
\Omega
(see Appendix
B
for relevant definitions).

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
I,I^{\prime}\subseteq\{0,\dots,k+s-1\}
be generating sets of
X
and
\mathcal{E}
be the set of
M
-congruent copies of
X
lying in
V(M-r)
.

(1)

We have that
\mathbb{L}_{I}(\Omega_{I})=\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}(\Omega_{I^{\prime}})
. In particular, the set
\Omega
is independent of the choice of the generating set, and is equal to the set of all almost
M
-congruent copies of
X
(with respect to every generating set of
X
).

(2)

If
d\geq(k+1)(k+2)+1,
then
\Omega_{I}
is a nice and consistent
M
-set of total co-dimension
(k+1)(k+2)/2
, and we have that
|\Omega|=|\Omega_{I}|=p^{d(k+1)-(k+1)(k+2)/2}(1+O_{k}(p^{-1/2}))
.

(3)

If
d\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+k,
then have that
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\Omega
with
|\Omega\backslash\mathcal{E}|=p^{d(k+1)-(k+1)(k+2)/2}O_{k}(p^{-1/2})
.

(4)

If
d\geq\max\{2k^{2}+6k+5,2k+s+13\}
, then for all
C^{\prime}>0
, there exists
K=O_{C^{\prime},d}(1)
such that
\Omega_{I}
admits a partially periodic
(t,Kt^{-K})
-Leibman dichotomy up to step
s
and complexity at most
C^{\prime}
for all
0<t<1/2
.

Proof.

We first prove Part (i).
Pick any
\mathbb{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}\in\Omega_{I^{\prime}}
. For all
i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
,
we have
M(x_{i}-x_{i^{\prime}})=M(v_{i}-v_{i^{\prime}})
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
.
Using the identity

2((x-y)A)\cdot(z-w)=M(x-w)+M(y-z)-M(x-z)-M(y-w),

we have that

((x_{i_{1}}-x_{i_{2}})A)\cdot(x_{i_{3}}-x_{i_{4}})=((v_{i_{1}}-v_{i_{2}})A)\cdot(v_{i_{3}}-v_{i_{4}})

for all
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\in I^{\prime}
. So for any
a_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p},i\in I^{\prime}
with
\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}}a_{i}=0
, we have that

M\Bigl(\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}}a_{i}x_{i}\Bigr)=M\Bigl(\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}\backslash\{i_{0}\}}a_{i}(x_{i}-x_{i_{0}})\Bigr)=M\Bigl(\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}\backslash\{i_{0}\}}a_{i}(v_{i}-v_{i_{0}})\Bigr)=M\Bigl(\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}}a_{i}v_{i}\Bigr),

where
i_{0}
is any element in
I^{\prime}
.
Therefore, for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
, we have that

M(\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime},i}(\mathbb{x})-\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(\mathbb{x}))=M\Bigl(\sum_{i^{\prime\prime}\in I^{\prime}}(b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime},i}-b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime},i^{\prime}})x_{i^{\prime\prime}}\Bigr)=M\Bigl(\sum_{i^{\prime\prime}\in I^{\prime}}(b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime},i}-b_{I^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime},i^{\prime}})v_{i^{\prime\prime}}\Bigr)=M(v_{i}-v_{i^{\prime}}).

On the other hand, since
M(x_{i})=r
for all
i\in I^{\prime}
and since
X
is spherical, it follows from Corollary
2.6
that
M(L_{I^{\prime},i}(\mathbb{x}))=r
for all
i\in I
. In conclusion, we have that
\pi_{I}(\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}(\mathbb{x}))
belongs to
\Omega_{I}
. Therefore, by Proposition
2.3
,
\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}(\mathbb{x})=\mathbb{L}_{I}(\pi_{I}(\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}(\mathbb{x})))\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{I}(\Omega_{I})
and so
\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}(\Omega_{I^{\prime}})\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{I}(\Omega_{I})
. Similarly, we have that
\mathbb{L}_{I}(\Omega_{I})\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}(\Omega_{I^{\prime}})
and so
\mathbb{L}_{I}(\Omega_{I})=\mathbb{L}_{I^{\prime}}(\Omega_{I^{\prime}})
.

We now prove Part (ii).
Assume without loss of generality
I=\{0,\dots,k\}
.
By part (i),
\Omega=\mathbb{L}_{I}(\Omega_{I})
. Since the projection of
\mathbb{L}_{I}
onto the coordinates with
I
-indices is the identity map on
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}
, we have that
|\Omega|=|\Omega_{I}|
.

Note that
\Omega_{I}
is the set
\Omega_{3}
in Example B.4 of
[
20
]
(up to a relabelling of the variables).
So
\Omega_{I}
is a nice and consistent
M
-set of total co-dimension
(k+1)(k+2)/2
.
Since
d\geq(k+1)(k+2)+1,
by Theorem
B.12
,
|\Omega_{I}|=p^{d(k+1)-\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}}(1+O_{k}(p^{-1/2}))
.

We then prove Part (iii). Since we have shown that
\Omega
is the set of all almost
M
-congruent copies of
X
in Part (i), it follows from Proposition
2.10
(i) that
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\Omega
. Moreover, by
Proposition
2.10
(ii), the set
\Omega\backslash\mathcal{E}
is a subset of
(x_{0},\dots,x_{k+s-1})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+s}
with
x_{i}-x_{i_{0}},i\in I\backslash\{i_{0}\}
being linearly independent for any prefixed generating set
I
and
i_{0}\in I
. So one can use Lemma
B.3
to see that
|\Omega\backslash\mathcal{E}|\leq kp^{dk+k-1}
. This completes the proof since
d\geq\frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}+k
.

Part (iv) follows from Theorem
B.15
and Part (ii).
∎

We conclude this section with a corollary of Proposition
3.4
.

Corollary 3.5
.

For any generating sets
I
and
I^{\prime}
of
X
, we have that
(\pi_{I}\circ L_{I^{\prime}})^{-1}(\Omega_{I})=\Omega_{I^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Since

b_{I^{\prime},i,j}=\delta_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in I^{\prime}
, we have that
\pi_{I^{\prime}}\circ L_{I^{\prime}}
is the identity map on
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}
. Similarly
\pi_{I}\circ L_{I}
is also the identity map.
So
for any
\mathbb{x}^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+1}
with
\pi_{I}\circ L_{I^{\prime}}(\mathbb{x}^{\prime})\in\Omega_{I}
, by Proposition
2.3
and Proposition
3.4
(i), we have that

\mathbb{x}^{\prime}=\pi_{I^{\prime}}\circ L_{I^{\prime}}(\mathbb{x}^{\prime})=\pi_{I^{\prime}}\circ L_{I}\circ\pi_{I}\circ L_{I^{\prime}}(\mathbb{x}^{\prime})\in\pi_{I^{\prime}}\circ L_{I}(\Omega_{I})=\pi_{I^{\prime}}\circ L_{I^{\prime}}(\Omega_{I^{\prime}})=\Omega_{I^{\prime}}.

Conversely, for any
\mathbb{x}^{\prime}\in\Omega_{I^{\prime}}
, by Proposition
3.4
(i), we have that

\pi_{I}\circ L_{I^{\prime}}(\mathbb{x})\in\pi_{I}\circ L_{I^{\prime}}(\Omega_{I^{\prime}})=\pi_{I}\circ L_{I}(\Omega_{I})=\Omega_{I}.

In conclusion, we have that
(\pi_{I}\circ L_{I^{\prime}})^{-1}(\Omega_{I})=\Omega_{I^{\prime}}
.
∎

4.
Bounding spherical averages by spherical Gowers norms

Theorem
3.1
is essentially equivalent to the positivity of the following spherical multiple ergodic average:

(4.1)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{(x_{0},\dots,x_{k+s-1})\in\Omega}f_{0}(x_{0})f_{1}(x_{1})\dots f_{k+s-1}(x_{k+s-1}),\end{split}

with
f_{0}=\dots=f_{k+s-1}
being the indicator function of
E
. This can be viewed as a variation of the by now conventional multiple ergodic average, where the set
\Omega
is the set of solutions to a linear equation system instead of a quadratic one. It turns out that (
4.1
) is connected to the spherical Gowers norms of
f_{0},\dots,f_{k+s-1}
, which we recall below:

Definition 4.1

(Shperical Gowers norms)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
and
f\colon\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
be a function. The
s
-th
V(M-r)
-Gowers norm
of
f
is defined by the quantity

\|f\|_{U^{s}(V(M-r))}:=\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{s})\in\Box_{s}(V(M-r))}\prod_{\epsilon=(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{s})\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\mathcal{C}^{|\epsilon|}f(n+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s}h_{s})\Bigr|^{\frac{1}{2^{s}}},

where

\Box_{s}(V(M-r))
is the
s
-th Gowers set of
V(M-r)
defined in Section
1.3
.
6
6

6

One can show that
\|\cdot\|_{U^{s}(V(M-r))}
is indeed a norm when
s\geq 2
.

To prove Theorem
3.1
,
our first step is to show that (
4.1
) can be bounded by the spherical Gowers norms of
f_{i}
.

Theorem 4.2

(Spherical generalized von-Neumann inequality)
.

If
d\geq(k+s+2)(k+s+1)+1
, then for any functions
f_{0},\dots,f_{k+s-1}\colon V(M-r)\to\mathbb{C}
bounded by 1, we have

(4.2)

\begin{split}|\mathbb{E}_{(x_{0},\dots,x_{k+s-1})\in\Omega}f_{0}(x_{0})f_{1}(x_{1})\dots f_{k+s-1}(x_{k+s-1})|\leq\|f_{0}\|_{U^{s}(V(M-r))}+O_{k,s}(p^{-O_{s}(1)^{-1}}).\end{split}

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
4.2
. A special case of Theorem
4.2
for
s=2
was proved in Proposition 6 of
[
18
]
, which inspires the proof of Theorem
4.2
in this paper.

Let
I_{0}\subseteq\{0,\dots,k+s-1\}
be any generating set of
X
which contains
0
. By symmetry, we may assume without loss of generality that
I_{0}=\{0,1,\dots,k\}
.
By Lemma
3.4
(i), the left hand side of (
4.2
) equals to

(4.3)

\begin{split}\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{k})\in\Omega_{I_{0}}}f_{0}(x_{0})\dots f_{k}(x_{k})\prod_{i=1}^{s-1}f_{k+i}(L_{I_{0},k+i}(\mathbb{x}))\Bigr|.\end{split}

By Lemma
3.4
(ii) and Theorem
B.12
, since
d\geq(k+2)(k+1)+1
, expression (
4.3
) can be bounded by

(4.4)

\begin{split}&\quad\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{y}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\Omega_{I_{0}\backslash\{0\}}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(x_{i})\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\in V(M-r)\cap U_{\mathbb{y}}}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{s-1}f_{k+i}(L_{I_{0},k+i}(x_{0},\mathbb{y}))\Bigr|+O_{k}(p^{-1/2})\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{y}\in\Omega_{I_{0}\backslash\{0\}}}\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\in V(M-r)\cap U_{\mathbb{y}}}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{s-1}f_{k+i}(L_{I_{0},k+i}(x_{0},\mathbb{y}))\Bigr|+O_{k}(p^{-1/2}),\end{split}

where
U_{\mathbb{y}}
is the set of
x\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
M(x-x_{i})=M(v_{0}-v_{i})
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.

We need to introduce some notations. For any subset
I
of
\{0,\dots,k+s-1\}
,
i_{0}\in I
and
t\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
let
\Omega^{i_{0}}_{I,t}
be the set of all
(\mathbb{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in I},\mathbb{h}=(h_{1},\dots,h_{t}))\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{|I|+t}
such that for all
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{t}\in\{0,1\}
we have that
(\tilde{x}_{i})_{i\in I}\in\Omega_{I}
, where
\tilde{x}_{i}:=x_{i}
for
i\in I\backslash\{i_{0}\}
and
\tilde{x}_{i_{0}}:=x_{i_{0}}+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{t}h_{t}
.
It is not hard to see that
\Omega_{I}
is a pure and consistent
M
-set. So
by Propositions
B.10
and
B.11
(vii),

(4.5)

\Omega^{i_{0}}_{I,t}
is a pure and consistent
M
-set of total co-dimension at most
\binom{|I|+t+1}{2}
.

Denote

\Delta_{h}f(L(x_{0},\mathbb{y})):=f(L(x_{0}+h,\mathbb{y}))\overline{f}(L(x_{0},\mathbb{y})).

Since
d\geq(k+3)(k+2)+1
,
by the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, (
4.5
) and Theorem
B.12
, the square of the right hand side of (
4.4
) is bounded by

(4.6)

\begin{split}&\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{y}\in\Omega_{I_{0}\backslash\{0\}}}\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\in V(M-r)\cap U_{\mathbb{y}}}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{s-1}f_{k+i}(L_{I_{0},k+i}(x_{0},\mathbb{y}))\Bigr|^{2}+O_{k}(p^{-1})\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{y}\in\Omega_{I_{0}\backslash\{0\}}}\mathbb{E}_{(x_{0},h)\in\Box_{1}(V(M-r)\cap U_{\mathbb{y}})}\Delta_{h}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{s-1}\Delta_{h}f_{k+i}(L_{I_{0},k+i}(x_{0},\mathbb{y}))+O_{k}(p^{-1})\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbb{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in I_{0}},h)\in\Omega^{0}_{I_{0},1}}\Delta_{h}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{s-1}\Delta_{h}f_{k+i}(L_{I_{0},k+i}(\mathbb{x}))+O_{k}(p^{-1}).\end{split}

For any generating set
I
of
X
with
0\in I
, any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, any
t\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and any
\mathbb{h}=(h_{1},\dots,h_{t})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{t}
, denote

\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f(L_{I,j}(x_{0},\mathbb{y}))=\Delta_{h_{t}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}f(L_{I,j}(x_{0},\mathbb{y}))=\prod_{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{t}\in\{0,1\}}\mathcal{C}^{\epsilon_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{t}+t}f(L_{I,j}(x_{0}+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{t}h_{t},\mathbb{y})),

i.e. the derivative
\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}
is taken with respect to the
0
-th variable of the function
f\circ L_{I,j}
. We need to prove the following intermediate estimate:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
1\leq t\leq s-1
and
I\subseteq\{0,\dots,k\}\cup\{k+t,\dots,k+s-1\}
be a generating set of
X
with
0\in I
. If
d\geq(k+s+2)(k+s+1)+1
, then there exists a generating set
I^{\prime}\subseteq\{0,\dots,k\}\cup\{k+t-1,\dots,k+s-1\}
of
X
with
0\in I
such that

(4.7)

\begin{split}&\quad\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbb{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in I},\mathbb{h})\in\Omega^{0}_{I,s-t}}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{t}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{k+i}(L_{I,k+i}(\mathbb{x}))\Bigr|^{2}\\
&\leq\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbb{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}},\mathbb{h})\in\Omega^{0}_{I^{\prime},s-t+1}}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{k+i}(L_{I^{\prime},k+i}(\mathbb{x}))\Bigr|+O_{k,s}(p^{-1/2}).\end{split}

We first explain how Proposition
4.3
implies Theorem
4.2
.
Since (
4.6
) holds, by repeatedly using Proposition
4.3
and the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, we have that
there exists a generating set
I
of
X
with
0\in I
such that the left hand side of (
4.2
) is bounded by

\begin{split}|\mathbb{E}_{((x_{i})_{i\in I},\mathbb{h})\in\Omega^{0}_{I,s}}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{0}(x_{0})|^{\frac{1}{2^{s}}}+O_{k,s}(p^{-O_{s}(1)^{-1}})\end{split}

Since
d\geq(k+s+2)(k+s+1)+1
,
by Theorem
B.12
and (
4.5
), this implies that

\begin{split}&\quad|\mathbb{E}_{((x_{i})_{i\in I},\mathbb{h})\in\Omega^{0}_{I,s}}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{0}(x_{0})|^{\frac{1}{2^{s}}}=|\mathbb{E}_{(x_{0},\mathbb{h})\in\Box_{s}(M-r)}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{0}(x_{0})|^{\frac{1}{2^{s}}}+O_{k,s}(p^{-O_{s}(1)^{-1}})\\
&=\|f_{0}\|_{U^{s}(M-r)}+O_{k,s}(p^{-O_{s}(1)^{-1}}).\end{split}

This completes the proof of Theorem
4.2
.
So it remains to prove Proposition
4.3
.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

By changing the order of the variables, we may assume without loss of generality that
I=\{0,\dots,k\}
.
Then

x_{k+t}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}b_{I,k+t,j}x_{j}=x_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}b_{I,k+t,j}(x_{j}-x_{0}).

So changing again the order of the variables if necessary, we may further assume that
b_{I,k+t,k}\neq 0
. Let
I^{\prime}=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t}\}
. Since
b_{I,k+t,k}\neq 0
, it is not hard to see that
I^{\prime}
is also a generating set of
X
.

The key to the proof of Proposition
4.3
is a change of variable that allows one to switch from the “
I
-basis” to the “
I^{\prime}
-basis”. Indeed,
unpacking the conclusion of Proposition
2.3
and using the fact that
b_{I,i,j}=\delta_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in I
, we deduce that

(4.8)

\begin{split}L_{I^{\prime},i}(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t})=L_{I,i}(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1},L_{I^{\prime},k}(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t}))\end{split}

for all

i\in\{k+1,\dots,k+s-1\}\backslash\{k+t\}
.
By the change of variable
x_{k}\mapsto L_{I^{\prime},k}(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t})
and (
4.8
), the left hand side of (
4.7
) is equal to

(4.9)

\begin{split}\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbb{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t}),\mathbb{h})\in W}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{0}(x_{0})\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{k+t}(x_{k+t})\prod_{i=0}^{t-1}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{k+i}(L_{I^{\prime},k+i}(\mathbb{x}))\Bigr|^{2},\end{split}

where
W
is the set of
(\mathbb{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t}),\mathbb{h}=(h_{1},\dots,h_{s-t}))\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+s}
such that

\begin{split}(x_{0}+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s-t}h_{s-t},x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1},L_{I^{\prime},k}(x_{0}+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s-t}h_{s-t},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t}))\in\Omega_{I}\end{split}

for all
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{s-t}\in\{0,1\}
, or equivalently,

\begin{split}(x_{0}+\epsilon_{1}h_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{s-t}h_{s-t},x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t})\in\pi_{I}\circ L_{I^{\prime}}^{-1}(\Omega_{I})=\Omega_{I^{\prime}},\end{split}

where we used (
4.8
) and Corollary
3.5
. In other words, we have that
W=\Omega^{0}_{I^{\prime},s-t}
. So (
4.9
) is equal to

(4.10)

\begin{split}\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbb{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t}),\mathbb{h})\in\Omega^{0}_{I^{\prime},s-t}}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{0}(x_{0})\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{k+t}(x_{k+t})\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{k+i}(L_{I^{\prime},k+i}(\mathbb{x}))\Bigr|^{2}.\end{split}

We refer the readers to Appendices
B.3
and
B.4
for the terminologies to be used in the discussion below.
By (
4.5
),
\Omega^{0}_{I^{\prime},s-t}
is a pure and consistent
M
-set of total co-dimension at most
\binom{k+s-t+2}{2}\leq\binom{k+s+1}{2}
.
So we may write
\Omega^{0}_{I^{\prime},s-t}=V(\mathcal{J})
for some consistent
(M,k+s-t+1)
-family
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}[x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t},h_{1},\dots,h_{s-t}]
of total dimension at most
\binom{k+s-t+2}{2}
. Let
(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime\prime})
be an
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t},h_{1},\dots,h_{s-t}\}
-decomposition of
\mathcal{J}
.
For convenience denote
\mathbb{y}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t})
.
Let
W^{\prime}
be the set of
(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+s-t}
such that
(x_{0},\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})\in V(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
for all
x_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
.
For
(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+s-t}
, let
\Omega^{0}_{I^{\prime},s-t}(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})
denote the set of
x_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
such that
(x_{0},\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})\in V(\mathcal{J}^{\prime\prime})
.
Since
d\geq(k+s+1)(k+s)+1
,
By Theorem
B.12
and the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, (
4.10
) is bounded by

(4.11)

\begin{split}&\quad\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})\in W^{\prime}}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{k+t}(x_{k+t})\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\in\Omega_{I^{\prime},s-t}^{0}(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{k+i}(L_{I^{\prime},k+i}(x_{0},\mathbb{y}))\Bigr|^{2}+O_{k,s}(p^{-1})\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})\in W^{\prime}}\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\in\Omega_{I^{\prime},s-t}^{0}(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{k+i}(L_{I^{\prime},k+i}(x_{0},\mathbb{y}))\Bigr|^{2}+O_{k,s}(p^{-1})\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})\in W^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0},x_{0}+h^{\prime}\in\Omega_{I^{\prime},s-t}^{0}(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})}\Delta_{h^{\prime}}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\Delta_{h^{\prime}}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}}f_{k+i}(L_{I^{\prime},k+i}(x_{0},\mathbb{y}))+O_{k,s}(p^{-1}).\end{split}

By (
4.5
),
\Omega^{0}_{I^{\prime},s-t+1}
is a consistent
M
-set of total co-dimension at most
\binom{k+s-t+3}{2}\leq\binom{k+s+2}{2}
. So we may write
\Omega^{0}_{I^{\prime},s-t+1}=V(\mathcal{J})
for some consistent
(M,k+s-t+2)
-family
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}[x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t},h_{1},\dots,h_{s-t},h^{\prime}]
of total dimension at most
\binom{k+s-t+3}{2}
. Let
(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime\prime})
be an
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1},x_{k+t},h_{1},\dots,h_{s-t}\}
-decomposition of
\mathcal{J}
.
It is not hard to compute that the set of
(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{k+s-t}
such that
(x_{0},\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h},h^{\prime})\in V(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
for all
x_{0},h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
is equal to
W^{\prime}
.
For
(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})\in W^{\prime}
and
(x_{0},h^{\prime})\in(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d})^{2}
, note that
(x_{0},\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h},h^{\prime})\in V(\mathcal{J}^{\prime\prime})
if and only if
x_{0},x_{0}+h\in\Omega_{I^{\prime},s-t}^{0}(\mathbb{y},\mathbb{h})
.
Write
\mathbb{h}^{\prime}:=(\mathbb{h},h^{\prime})
.
By Theorem
B.12
, since
d\geq(k+s+2)(k+s+1)+1
, the right hand side of (
4.11
) is bounded by

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbb{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}},\mathbb{h}^{\prime})\in\Omega^{0}_{I^{\prime},s-t+1}}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}^{\prime}}f_{0}(x_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\Delta_{\mathbb{h}^{\prime}}f_{k+i}(L_{I^{\prime},k+i}(\mathbb{x}))+O_{k,s}(p^{-1/2}).\end{split}

where
\mathbb{h}^{\prime}:=(\mathbb{h},h^{\prime})
. This completes the proof of Proposition
4.3
.
∎

5.
The rationality of polynomial sequences

5.1.
Type-III horizontal torus and character

We refer the readers to Appendix
A
for the definitions and properties for nilmanifolds defined in
[
20
]
. In this paper, in addition to the type-I and type-II horizontal toruses and characters defined in
[
20
]
and
[
22
]
respectively, we need to introduce a third type of horizontal torus and character.

Definition 5.1

(
i
-th type-III horizontal torus and character)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of degree at most
s
with a filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
and a Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
.
Let
G_{i}^{\nabla}
denote the group generated by
G_{i+1}
and
[G_{j},G_{i-j}]
for all
0\leq j\leq i
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
G_{i}^{\nabla}
is a subgroup of
G_{i}
. It is not hard to check that
(G_{i}^{\nabla})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is an
\mathbb{N}
-filtration. We say that
G_{i}/G_{i}^{\nabla}(\Gamma\cap G_{i})

is the
i
-th type-III horizontal torus
of
G/\Gamma
.

An
i
-th type-III horizontal character
of
G/\Gamma
is a continuous homomorphism
\eta_{i}\colon G_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
which vanishes on
G_{i}^{\nabla}
, and maps
\Gamma\cap G_{i}
to
\mathbb{Z}
. We say that
\eta_{i}
is
nontrivial
if it is not constant zero.
The Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
induces a natural isomorphism
\psi\colon G_{i}/G_{i+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and thus we may write
\eta_{i}(g_{i}):=(m_{1},\dots,m_{k})\cdot\psi(g_{i})
for some
m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
k=\dim(G_{i})-\dim(G_{i+1})
.
We call the quantity
\|\eta_{i}\|:=|m_{1}|+\dots+|m_{k}|
the
complexity
of
\eta_{i}
(with respect to
\mathcal{X}
).

Type-III horizontal torus and character are used to characterize the rationality property of a nilsequence (see for example
[
2
,
9
]
), which is a property more delicate than the equidistribution property.

We provide a proposition for type-III horizontal characters for later uses.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of degree at most
s
with a filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
g,g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to G_{\mathbb{N}})
,
1\leq i\leq s
, and
\eta_{i}\colon G_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
be an
i
-th type-III horizontal character of
G/\Gamma
.

(i) Let

g(n)=\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{d},0\leq|j|\leq s}g_{j}^{\binom{n}{j}}

be the type-I Taylor expansion of
g
, where
g_{j}\in G_{|j|}
. Then for all
h_{1},\dots,h_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n))
is well defined and is equal to

\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}\Bigl(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|j|=i}\eta_{i}(g_{j}){\binom{n}{j}}\Bigr)
.

(ii) For all
h_{1},\dots,h_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have that

\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n))+\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g^{\prime}(n))=\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}gg^{\prime}(n)).

Proof.

We first prove Part (i).
\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n))
is well defined since
\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n)\in G_{i}
. Since
\eta_{i}
vanishes on
G_{i}^{\nabla}
, by repeatedly using Corollary B.7 of
[
12
]
, it is not hard to see that

\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n)\equiv\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|j|=i}g_{j}^{\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}\binom{n}{j}}\mod G^{\nabla}_{|i|}.

So

\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n))=\eta_{i}\Bigl(\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|j|=i}g_{j}^{\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}\binom{n}{j}}\Bigr)=\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}\Bigl(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|j|=i}\eta_{i}(g_{j}){\binom{n}{j}}\Bigr).

We now prove Part (ii). By the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula (see Appendix
A.3
) and Corollary B.7 of
[
12
]
, it is not hard to see that the
i
-th type-I Taylor coefficient of
gg^{\prime}
equals to that of
g
multiplied by that of
g^{\prime}
modulo
G^{\nabla}_{|i|}
. The conclusion then follows from Part (i) and the linearity of
\eta_{i}\mod G^{\nabla}_{|i|}
.
∎

5.2.
The rationality version of the factorization theorem

As was explain the paper of Green and Tao
[
9
]
, the type-I horizontal characters and the factorization theorem pertaining to them are insufficient for the study of the equidistribution property for Leibman groups (see Section
6
for the definition).
For this reason, we need to upgrade the equidistribution result obtained in the previous part of the series
(i.e. Theorems 11.2 of
[
20
]
)
for type-III horizontal characters. We start with the following concept.

Definition 5.3

(Irrationality)
.

Let
d,s\in\mathbb{N}_{+},N>0
,
p
be a prime,
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of degree at most
s
with a filtration
G_{\mathbb{N}}
and a Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}
,
\Omega
be a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. We say that
g
is
(N,\Omega,p)
-rational
there exist
1\leq i\leq s
and a nontrivial
i
-th type-III horizontal character
\eta_{i}\colon G_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
of complexity at most
N
such that the map

(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{i})\mapsto\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g(n))\mod\mathbb{Z}

(which is well defined by Proposition
5.2
) is a constant on
\Box_{p,i}(\Omega)
(recall Section
1.3
for the definition).
We say that
g
is
(N,\Omega,p)
-irrational
if it is not
(N,\Omega,p)
-rational.

Let
\Omega
be a subset of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
(recall the definitions of different notions of polynomial sequences in Appendices
A.1
and
A.2
). We say that
g
is
(N,\Omega)
-rational/irrational
if
g=g^{\prime}\circ\tau
for some
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
which is
(N,\iota^{-1}(\Omega),p)
-rational/irrational.

For convenience, we define every polynomial of degree
s=0
(i.e. every constant polynomial) to be
(N,\iota^{-1}(\Omega),p)
-irrational or
(N,\Omega)
-irrational for all
N>0
.

The next lemma says that the rationality of a polynomial in
\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
is independent of choice of the representatives in
\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\Omega
be a subset of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
and
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.
If for some
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
with
g=g^{\prime}\circ\tau
,
g^{\prime}
is
(N,\iota^{-1}(\Omega),p)
-rational/irrational, then for every
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
with
g=g^{\prime}\circ\tau
, we have that
g^{\prime}
is
(N,\iota^{-1}(\Omega),p)
-rational/irrational. In other words, the
(N,\Omega)
-rational/irrational property of
g
is independent of the choices of
g^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
G/\Gamma
is of degree at least 1.
Let
g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)

with
g=g^{\prime}\circ\tau=g^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau
. It suffices to show that for any
i
-th type-III horizontal character
\eta_{i}\colon G_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
and any
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{i})\in\Box_{p,i}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega))
, we have that

(5.1)

\begin{split}\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g^{\prime\prime}(n))\equiv\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g^{\prime}(n))\mod\mathbb{Z}.\end{split}

Write
\tilde{g}:={g^{\prime}}^{-1}g^{\prime\prime}
. Since
g^{\prime}\circ\tau=g^{\prime\prime}\circ\tau
, we have that
g^{\prime}(n)^{-1}g^{\prime\prime}(n)\in\Gamma
for all
n\in\tau(\Omega)
. Since
g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{I}|\Gamma)
, for all
n\in\tau(\Omega)
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we have that

g^{\prime}(n+pm)^{-1}g^{\prime\prime}(n+pm)=(g^{\prime}(n+pm)^{-1}g^{\prime}(n))(g^{\prime}(n)^{-1}g^{\prime\prime}(n))(g^{\prime\prime}(n)^{-1}g^{\prime\prime}(n+pm))\in\Gamma.

So

\tilde{g}\in\text{poly}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
.

By Proposition
5.2
,

(5.2)

\begin{split}\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g^{\prime\prime}(n))-\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}g^{\prime}(n))=\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}\tilde{g}(n)).\end{split}

For any
(n,h_{1},\dots,h_{i})\in\Box_{p,i}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega))
,
since
\tilde{g}\in\text{poly}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
,
\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}\tilde{g}(n)
belongs to
\Gamma
. So we have that

\eta_{i}(\Delta_{h_{i}}\dots\Delta_{h_{1}}\tilde{g}(n))\in\mathbb{Z}
and thus (
5.1
) follows from (
5.2
).
∎

Let
\Omega
be a non-empty finite set and
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold. We say that a sequence
\mathcal{O}\colon\Omega\to G/\Gamma
is
\delta
-equidistributed
on
G/\Gamma
if for all Lipschitz function
F\colon G/\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
, we have that

\Bigl|\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{n\in\Omega}F(\mathcal{O}(n))-\int_{G/\Gamma}F\,dm_{G/\Gamma}\Bigr|\leq\delta\|F\|_{\text{Lip}},

where
m_{G/\Gamma}
is the Haar measure of
G/\Gamma
.
7
7

7

This definition is slight different from the one used in
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
, where
\Omega
is a (possibly infinite) subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

The next lemma explains the connection between irrationality and equidistribution of polynomial sequences. We do not use it in this paper, but it is interesting on its own.

Lemma 5.5

(Irrationality implies equidistribution)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+},s\in\mathbb{N},C,C^{\prime},K,N>0
,
p
be a prime,
and
G/\Gamma
be an
\mathbb{N}
-filtered nilmanifold of degree at most
s
and complexity at most
C
. There exists
C^{\prime\prime}=C^{\prime\prime}(C,C^{\prime},K,s)>0
such that for any

\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{d}
which admits a partially periodic
(\delta,C^{\prime}\delta^{-K})
-Leibman dichotomy
8
8

8

See Appendix
B.7
for the definition.
up to step
s
and complexity
C
and any
(N,\Omega)
-irrational
g\in\text{poly}_{p}(\Omega\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
, the sequence
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega}
is

C^{\prime\prime}N^{-1/K}
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
.

Proof.

There is nothing to prove when
s=0
, and so we assume that
s\geq 1
.
Let
\delta>0
to be chosen later. Assume that
g=g^{\prime}\circ\tau
for some
g^{\prime}\in\text{poly}_{p}(\iota^{-1}(\Omega)\to G_{\mathbb{N}}|\Gamma)
. Suppose that
(g(n)\Gamma)_{n\in\Omega}
is not
\delta
-equidistributed on
G/\Gamma
, then by assumption, there exists a nontrivial type-I horizontal character
\eta\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
of complexity at most
C^{\prime}\delta^{-K}
such that
